- **Tests** (each was run before its fix and failed; the pin's check and the DMS check were each removed once to see their tests fail, and the DMS check with "either coordinate" or with 0.15" fails a control): web unit `dispatch-location-input.spec.ts` +24 (Google's pin with fewer than 4 decimals: the address Google opens, one decimal, one coordinate, through a consent page, a 4-decimal search ending in 0, a short link; the DMS of a rough pair as Google shows it, with primes, in whole seconds, in `/place/`, `?q=` and at the end of a directions link, whole degrees with seconds; neither asks to confirm the pin; controls: a real 7-decimal marker, a 4-decimal pin, real DMS in tenths and whole seconds, a tenth of a second off, the highest seconds, a searched 4-decimal pair, one coordinate alone, a real point in `/place/`), `location-rule-routes.spec.ts` +4 (the Read of the link, the link without its data part, the DMS text and the short link; PUT refused however it is sent, then a hand pin saved; a real marker and real DMS read and saved as read). Tests changed, and why: `dispatch-location-input.spec.ts` "still reads the highest valid minutes and seconds" expected 23°00'00"N 58°00'00.0"E (whole degrees written with seconds) to read HIGH; it now needs a pin. Two control labels said Google's pin keeps its digits because nobody types it; they now say its zeros are not cut. The script was run on a throwaway database, with writes refused (`default_transaction_read_only`), on 11 fictional customers: 8 confirmed from a pasted reading, 5 read as not exact (1 padded pair, 2 with fewer decimals, one of them Google's pin, 1 DMS, 1 map centre), 1 short link, as seeded. The two database-level suites that read locations pass. Counts: [5.3](#53-test-suites).
- **Not in this part:** flagging the customers the count finds (the owner's decision); DMS written by another tool in whole seconds from a rough value it rounded (23.581 as 23°34'52"N) is not recognised as rough; a search with one rough coordinate pasted as Google's DMS text (above).

**A5 seventh review fixes** (same branch, `audit-5-owner-rules`, on `0d90b12`). One finding, confirmed by two skeptics on their own throwaway databases. No new or changed API route (the role matrix is unchanged), no schema or migration change, no solver change, no change a dispatcher can see; only the owner's read-only count and its words.

- *The owner's count called customers placed on the map "confirmed from a pasted reading ... not exact today"* (medium). On main the customer page saved a map click or two typed numbers through `PATCH /api/customers/:id` with `{ lat, lng }`: source `MANUAL_LATLNG`, confirmed, HIGH, and `locationInput` not touched, so it stayed empty or kept an older ADD LOCATION text for another point. `prisma/check-saved-readings.ts` selected only the text and read it without the saved point, so a precise map click with no text was counted under "another reason ... not readable", a map click after an old padded text under "the zeros at the end", and the saved numbers RouteIQ writes when a saved point is confirmed as it is (`${lat}, ${lng}`, "23.585, 58.4123") under "fewer than 4 decimals", all in the one total that 5.12 step 13 and the 7.5 question ask the owner to use to decide whether to flag customers. Flagged on that count, exact map-clicked customers would stop being planned until a dispatcher placed a hand pin. A skeptic's six made-up customers printed "6 confirmed from a pasted reading; 4 read as not exact today"; one was. **Fixed:** the script reads the saved point with the text (`classifySavedReading`) and judges a text only when the saved point was read from it: the text reads back to the saved point, to the 6 decimals the parser keeps (`samePoint`), or it holds the saved point as two numbers but reads otherwise today, which means it was read differently before A5 (for example a directions link main read at its start: counted, with that reason). The others are listed apart, "not judged by their text", each on its own line: no text kept (a map click or typed numbers on the customer page before A5), a text that reads as another point (the point set after it; or, before audit A2, the box edited after Read), the saved numbers as RouteIQ writes them when they read as not exact (a stored 23.5850 is written "23.585", a swapped point confirmed as it was is written swapped; a pair typed exactly so looks the same, so it is not counted either way), and a text that cannot be read and does not hold the saved point ("map pin"); with how many of them have fewer than 4 decimals as stored (a rough pair typed on the customer page, or an exact 4-decimal value ending in 0). A customer with no saved point is not read. The same six customers now print "2 confirmed from a pasted reading ...; 1 read as not exact today" (the padded reading) and "4 not judged by their text ...; 2 with fewer than 4 decimals as stored". The header line, 5.12 step 13 and the 7.5 question now say that only the "read as not exact today" count is the question.
- **Deviations** (each with its reason): the saved numbers as RouteIQ writes them are apart only when they read as not exact; an exact one counts as an exact reading, since a pair copied or typed in that form (for example "23.601234, 58.412345") cannot be told from RouteIQ's own and is exact whoever wrote it. A text that holds the saved point but reads otherwise today is counted as a pasted reading (not "another point"): the finding's rule, "reads back to the saved point", would have listed a directions link main read at its start as a point set on the map, though the dispatcher pasted it and the saved point is the start of the route. The count of "fewer than 4 decimals as stored" is for the customers not judged by their text only, so the owner sees the rough points typed on the customer page before A5 (the reviewers' fifth customer, counted nowhere before) without calling them pasted readings. The pure parts are exported from the script, which runs only when started as a script (the pattern of `grant-platform-admin.ts`).
- **Tests** (each was run before its fix and failed: against the old script, the reviewers' six customers printed the old "6 confirmed ...; 4 read as not exact today" lines; the point comparison, the saved-numbers line, the "holds the saved point" rule and the selected saved point were each removed once to see their tests fail): web unit `check-saved-readings.spec.ts` +6, new (the reviewers' six customers; a map click with no text; each kind of row alone; a directions link that holds the saved point, with and without a map centre and in the other order; the lines and reasons of the report; customers not confirmed, placed by hand or with no point not read). The script was run, before and after, on a throwaway database with writes refused (`default_transaction_read_only`), migrated with this commit's migrations, on 10 made-up customers: before, "10 confirmed from a pasted reading; 7 read as not exact today" (a map click with no text, a map click after a map-centre link and "map pin" among them); after, 3 confirmed from a pasted reading, 2 not exact (the padded pair, the directions link read at its start), 1 short link, 6 not judged by their text (2 with fewer than 4 decimals as stored). Counts: [5.3](#53-test-suites).
- **Not in this part:** which customers on the "not judged" lines have a rough point (the script names no customer; the owner looks them up on the map, as for the unconfirmed HIGH query in step 13); a text read differently before A5 whose saved point is not written in it as decimals (degrees and minutes of 60 or more, read before audit F17) is listed as "cannot be read", not counted.

**Audit P5 "Uploads read in a separate process"** (branch `audit-p5-upload-worker`, on main `3451f4d`, which has A1 to A5; assessment PR 5, E2's proper fix, owner decision 16; called P5 because "A5" already names the owner-rules PR). The owner's design: parse every upload in an isolated worker with a heap cap and a time limit that really stops it, a few at once, 503 "busy" beyond, refusals in plain words, behaviour otherwise unchanged, working in `next dev`, the tests and `next build` + `next start` as Railway runs it.

- **What was wrong (E2).** Since A1 the caps bound one upload's work, but the read still ran in the web process, synchronously: nothing could stop it, no other request was answered meanwhile, and its memory was the web process's. Measured on main before P5 (table below, this PC under full CPU load): a legal file near the caps froze every user for 9-45 s and took the web process to 0.6-1.3 GB.
- **What changed.**
  - The three upload routes (order file and late orders `POST /api/orders/upload`, customer import, baseline) call `parseUploadIsolated` (`lib/upload-parse/index.ts`) instead of `parseUpload`. It checks the size and type first (`fileRefusal`, `lib/upload-limits.ts`, the same messages), then forks one fresh Node process per file (`child.ts`, bundled) with `--max-old-space-size=UPLOAD_WORKER_MAX_HEAP_MB` (512), sends it the bytes, and kills it (SIGKILL) after `UPLOAD_PARSE_TIMEOUT_MS` (15,000). The process runs the unchanged `parseUpload` (`handler.ts`) and answers the rows in pieces of about 25,000 cells, then the reply (`protocol.ts`): the web process turns each piece into objects as it arrives, so it answers other requests in between. Errors come back as data and are thrown again with the same message; a `MultipleSheetsError` or `WorkbookRefusedError` is rebuilt as one (both moved to the dependency-free `lib/upload-errors.ts`; `lib/csv.ts` and `lib/workbook-guard.ts` re-export them).
  - Refusals (`UploadParseRefused`, answered by `uploadRefusedResponse` as `{ data: null, error }`): too long 400 "This file takes too long to read, so it was not read. Nothing was saved. Delete the rows and columns you do not need (also empty rows below your data), or split it into smaller files. Save as .xlsx or CSV and upload again."; too much memory 400, the same advice ("needs too much memory"; V8's "heap out of memory" abort, exit 134 or SIGABRT, or a SIGKILL RouteIQ did not send, which is the kernel's out-of-memory killer); the reader stopped 500; the reader missing 500 ("tell your administrator"); busy 503 with Retry-After 5 "RouteIQ is reading other files right now. Try again in a moment.". Nothing is written before the read succeeds (the batch, the customers, the baseline and their audit rows all come after it).
  - At most `UPLOAD_PARSE_CONCURRENCY` (2) parser processes per web process; a further upload waits up to 5 s (`QUEUE_WAIT_MS`; at most 8 wait, `MAX_WAITING`), then gets 503. A slot is freed when its process has closed (a kill that is not followed by an exit frees it after 10 s, logged). The state lives on `globalThis`, so `next dev` reloads share it.
  - The parser process gets no secrets (its environment is `NODE_ENV`, the time zone, locale and system paths; Windows adds its required user and system variables), none of the web's Node flags, and on Linux raises its own `oom_score_adj` to 1000, so the kernel kills it before the web process when the container runs out of memory. Each read is logged (`[upload-parse] read <MB> MB in <ms> ms, parser peak <MB> MB (process <pid>): <n> rows`; refusals as warnings).
  - The route passes what the upload is for as data (`ParseSpec`: `orderSheet.extraAliases`, `rowsWord`, `decimalTextColumns`), since a function cannot cross to another process; the parser builds the order-sheet test from it with `isOrderSheet`, which moved with the header aliases to `lib/dispatch/order-headers.ts` (no dependencies; `order-intake.ts` re-exports them) so the parser bundles no zod or schemas.
  - **Build.** `pnpm build` is now `prisma generate && next build && node scripts/build-upload-parser.mjs && node scripts/remove-public-source-maps.mjs`: esbuild (new dev dependency `^0.27.7`, already in the lockfile through tsx; only the importer changed) bundles `lib/upload-parse/child.ts` into `.next/upload-parser/parse.cjs` (835 KB, 10 inputs, no source map, not under `.next/static`). The build fails unless the bundle holds only the parser's own modules, SheetJS's `xlsx.mjs` (the build Next.js bundled for the web and vitest loads; `xlsx.js` would add the codepage tables) and Papa Parse (`scripts/upload-parser-build.cjs`). `next start` (run in `apps/web`, as Railway's `pnpm --filter @routeiq/web start` does) forks it. `next dev` and the tests fork `scripts/upload-parser-dev.cjs`, which builds the same bundle into `node_modules/.cache/routeiq-upload-parser` when a source changed, so webpack and vitest never load esbuild. The CI build check (`check-build-output.ts`) also fails when the bundle is missing, holds anything else or has a map beside it, and runs it once on a two-line CSV. A production server reads a test CSV in a parser process at startup (`checkUploadParser`, `instrumentation.ts`) and logs `[upload-parse] the file reader works (startup check)` or a `[config]` error; `lib/startup-checks.ts` warns on an `UPLOAD_*` value that is not usable. A1's no-public-source-maps and no-Server-Actions checks are unchanged and pass.
  - The web process no longer loads SheetJS at all: the SheetJS chunk is gone from `.next/server` (checked in the build).
  - **Tests** read uploads in-process by default (`tests/setup.ts`, honoured outside production only, the same request and piecewise answer objects), so every existing spec that watches or replaces SheetJS passed unchanged; the new specs start the real process.
- **Measured before (main `3451f4d`) and after**, over HTTP: each file on a fresh `next start -p 3008` (a two-line warm-up upload first), `/api/health/live` asked back to back every 10 ms during the upload; the order files through `POST /api/orders/upload` (answered 422 "no active depot" right after the read), the others through the customer import's Validate only. This Windows PC was at 100% CPU with other work the whole time, so times are long and noisy (the same 50,000 x 20 file took 13-19 s whether read in a process or in-process). Real files: counts and timings only.

  | File | Before: answer, time, longest `/health/live` wait, web process peak | After: answer, time, longest wait, web peak; parser process peak |
  |---|---|---|
  | NMWC's real order files (.xlsx of 373 and 345 rows, .csv of 373 rows, also as Excel, late orders) | read; 0.3-0.9 s; 47-308 ms; 164-171 MB | read; 0.5-0.8 s; 76-255 ms; 165-167 MB; parser 65-72 MB, 0.25-0.45 s |
  | NMWC's customer file (80 rows) | read; 0.6 s; 178 ms; 165 MB | read; 0.8 s; 315 ms; 165 MB; parser 63 MB |
  | A1's crafted files that are refused (64 MB unpacked, typed empty cells, one part named ten times, metadata, comment authors, .xls link, CFB chain) | refused; 0.2-1.3 s; up to 1.0 s; up to 256 MB | refused; 0.6-2.0 s; up to 277 ms; up to 176 MB; parser up to 145 MB |
  | 10,000 comments (read) | 0.6 s; 380 ms; 186 MB | 3.0 s; 265 ms; 176 MB; parser 76 MB |
  | 45 MB unpacked, 1.1 million rows (refused "Too many rows") | 9.9 s; 9.6 s; 438 MB | 5.5 s; 483 ms; 197 MB; parser 358 MB |
  | A 255-character number format on 50,000 x 44 cells | read after 44.8 s; 44.3 s; 1,094 MB | refused "takes too long" at 15.6 s; 372 ms; 232 MB |
  | 50,000 rows x 20 / 30 / 40 / 49 columns (.xlsx, 0.16-0.22 MB) | read; 8.6 / 21.5 / 33.1 / 36.9 s; the same; 599 / 718 / 832 / 947 MB | refused "takes too long" at 15.2-16.3 s; up to 811 ms; up to 259 MB |
  | NMWC's shape at the row limit, 50,000 x 15 (2.9 MB) | read 14.0 s; 13.4 s; 537 MB | refused "takes too long" at 15.5 s; 484 ms; 216 MB |
  | Ten sheets of 50,000 x 4 | read 29.7 s; 28.5 s; 707 MB | refused "takes too long" at 15.5 s; 306 ms; 230 MB |
  | 50,000 x 49 as CSV (4.7 MB) | read 5.0 s; 4.4 s; 614 MB | read 4.6 s; 264 ms; 242 MB; parser 504 MB, 4.3 s |
  | The same CSV sent as Excel | read 21.7 s; 21.1 s; 1,274 MB | refused "takes too long" at 15.2 s; 159 ms; 265 MB |

  Three NMWC-shape files at once (2 parser processes): two read until the time limit, the third answered 503 with Retry-After 5 after 6.0 s; `/health/live` answered 717 times, median 3 ms, 99th percentile 45 ms, longest 354 ms. A slow file under a 3 s limit: refused at 4.6 s, longest wait 639 ms. What the web process still does on its own event loop, measured in the test process: starting a process (80-260 ms on this PC under load, up to about 700 ms at worst; on Linux, where Railway runs, a few ms, not measured here), turning the rows into objects (with pieces the longest stall at the end of a 750,000-cell read went from 697 ms to 102 ms, of a 2,450,000-cell read from 1,845 ms to under 120 ms; corrected in the P5 second review: that holds for rows within the caps, but a row is never cut into pieces and a CSV sent as text had no column cap, so one CSV row of a million columns still blocked the web process for 1.1-1.6 s; such a CSV is refused since then), and the route's own work on the rows (the order check, the customer loop), which is unchanged. Without load the same files read in 0.3 s (NMWC's), 4-6 s (NMWC's shape at the row limit) and 6-17.5 s (50,000 x 20-49), per the read-only mapping probe on this PC.
- **`next dev` and `next start`, checked by hand.** The integration suite (all 30 files, 257 tests) passed against the built app under `next start`, whose startup check logged `[upload-parse] the file reader works (startup check)`. Under `next dev` NMWC's order file (373 rows) was read by the parser in 0.5 s and the customer file in 0.3 s, and a slow legal file was refused after 4.7 s while `/health/live` answered within 0.5 s. (Corrected in the P5 review.) This block said that `next dev` answers 500 on every API route in this worktree. That was wrong: those runs started `next` directly (`node node_modules/next/dist/bin/next dev`), without pnpm's launcher, and then Sentry's route wrapper cannot find `@sentry/core` ([5.2](#52-local-development)). Started as documented (`pnpm --filter @routeiq/web dev`), `next dev` answers 200 in this worktree, and the `next dev` check, which was run with Sentry's wrapper left out of `next.config.js`, did not need that.
- **Leaks.** Tested: 40 reads, kills and crashes leave no process, slot, handle or process listener (`upload-parse-process.spec.ts`). A long run (own `.dev` script, not in CI): 3,000 parses in 20 minutes, two at a time, with every 50th a parser that loops (killed at a 1.5 s limit) and every 50th one that crashes (2,748 read, 198 killed at the limit on the busy PC, 55 crashed): after each 250 the parent's heap after a full GC stayed at 9-11 MB, its RSS at 112-117 MB, its active handles at 0-1, with no parser process left.
- **Deviations** (each with its reason):
  - *A separate process (`child_process.fork`), not a worker thread.* A worker's heap cap does not always end only the worker: with the legal 0.22 MB file of 50,000 rows x 49 columns and a 256 MB cap, V8 aborted the whole process ("FATAL ERROR: CALL_AND_RETRY_LAST Allocation failed - JavaScript heap out of memory", exit 134), on Node 22.13.1 and 24.14.1 (the mapping probe; checked again for this block). The same file in a child process with the same cap ended the child only. The variable names stay the owner's (`UPLOAD_WORKER_MAX_HEAP_MB` included). A kill really frees everything, and one fresh process per file shares nothing.
  - *The rows come back in pieces.* Not in the design: in one message, turning 2.45 million cells back into objects blocked the web process for up to 1.8 s on this PC.
  - *The tests read in-process by default* (`tests/setup.ts`), so the existing specs that spy on or replace SheetJS keep working unchanged; the identity spec proves the real process gives the same answers.
  - *Status codes*: a stopped or missing reader is 500 (a server problem), too long and too much memory 400 (the file), busy 503.
  - *The queue wait (5 s) and length (8) are constants*, not variables.
  - *Not asked for*: the production startup check and the per-read log line (so a broken reader shows at the deploy and the memory can be sized from real peaks).
  - *Not done*: killing the parser when the browser gives up (the time limit bounds it); capping the request body before `req.formData()` (below).
- **Behaviour changes a dispatcher can see.** The new refusals above. A file that needs more than 512 MB or 15 s is refused instead of read: the widest legal files (50,000 rows of more than about 45 columns, or 50,000 x 49 sent as Excel CSV) need 650-900 MB and are refused on any machine; on a slow or busy CPU, files near the caps may reach 15 s (on this PC under load every one did, NMWC's shape at the row limit included). Each upload takes about 0.2-0.5 s longer on this PC (starting the process). Every other answer, message, warning, row number and sheet choice is the same (`upload-parse-identity.spec.ts`).
- **Tests** (each guarantee checked to fail without it, by a script that takes one mechanism out at a time and runs only its tests; all 11 failed as they should: the heap cap, the kill at the time limit, the time limit, the parser's own environment, the concurrency limit, production ignoring the tests' in-process reading, the size and type check before a process starts, the pieces, the `MultipleSheetsError` rebuilt, the orders route reading in the web process, a route answering a refusal as a plain 400): `upload-parse-identity.spec.ts` (56), `upload-parse-process.spec.ts` (23, one Linux only), `upload-parse-routes.spec.ts` (13), `upload-isolation.spec.ts` (integration, 2), `build-hardening.spec.ts` + 5, `repo-guards.spec.ts` + 4 (and the status-line guard reads "Audit P5"), `ops-config.spec.ts` + 1; the build script pin changed on purpose. Counts: [5.3](#53-test-suites).
- **Still open / owner.** Check the web service's memory on Railway before the deploy ([5.6](#56-deployment-on-railway); about 1.3 GB more while two large files are read); decide the defaults and the request-body cap ([7.5](#75-open-questions-for-the-owner)); deploy check [5.12](#512-how-to-verify-a-deploy) step 14. If the web process dies during a read, the parser runs to the end of that read (bounded by its heap cap and the caps) and then exits. `req.formData()` still reads a whole upload request into the web process before the 10 MB check (an older gap, not E2).

**P5 review fixes** (same branch, `audit-p5-upload-worker`, on `93d2331`). Three findings, each confirmed by two skeptics: one medium (code) and two low (handbook). No new or changed API route (the role matrix is unchanged), no schema or migration change, no solver change, no new setting.

- *The parser's answer had no size limit, so a small file could still fill the web process* (medium). Node's IPC writes a text out again for every place it appears, and the web process got its own copy of each. In the parser, the cells that show one shared string of an .xlsx all point at one text; after the answer crossed, every cell had its own copy. The pieces were cut by cells only, and the web process kept every piece with no limit. So a 6 KB .xlsx within every cap (50,000 cells that show one 4,096-character shared string) came back as 196 MB and held 199 MB of the web process's heap, where `parseUpload` in the web process (main) had held one text. In a skeptic's run with the web side's heap capped at 128 MB, the web side itself died ("heap out of memory") while the parser ended normally: the one thing P5 is there to prevent. **Fixed** in `lib/upload-parse/protocol.ts`:
  - *Every text crosses once.* The parser sends the rows as numbers into one list of the answer's texts (each piece first carries the texts that are new in it), and the web process builds the rows from that list. A text many cells show, or a header, is then one text in the web process too, as it was on main.
  - *The answer is at most 128 MB* (`MAX_RESULT_BYTES`). The parser turns each message into bytes itself (`v8.serialize`), cuts a piece at 25,000 cells or once its new texts pass about 4 MB, and sends no more than the limit in all: an answer that would pass it ends with a `too-large` message instead, and the file is refused with the existing "This file needs too much memory to read, so it was not read. ..." (400, `UPLOAD_OUT_OF_MEMORY`).
  - *The web process counts too.* `ReplyCollector` adds up the bytes of each message before it turns them into objects; past the limit it refuses the file the same way and the parser is killed. The request carries the limit, so both sides use one number. The CI build check reads its smoke test's answer with the same `ReplyCollector`.
- *Measured* (this PC; own `.dev` scripts; before = the parser and web side of `93d2331`, after = this fix; "held" is the web side's heap after a full GC while the rows are kept; real files: counts and timings only):

  | File | Before: answer, web heap held, parser peak | After: answer, web heap held, parser peak |
  |---|---|---|
  | The review's file: 50,000 cells of one 4,096-character shared string (5.8 KB) | 196 MB; 199 MB; 103 MB | 0.58 MB; 3.1 MB; 115 MB |
  | 50,000 x 2 cells of one 2,048-character text (9.6 KB) | 196 MB; 198 MB; 131 MB | 0.96 MB; 3.1 MB; 135 MB |
  | NMWC's real files (orders .xlsx and .csv of 373 rows, the .csv sent as Excel, late orders, customers, products, trucks) | at most 0.09 MB; under 1 MB; 64-72 MB | at most 0.03 MB; under 1 MB; 65-73 MB |
  | NMWC's shape at the row limit, 50,000 x 15 (2.9 MB) | 14.1 MB; 24.8 MB; 398 MB | 6.4 MB; 10.0 MB; 448 MB |
  | 50,000 x 49 as CSV (4.7 MB) | 18.5 MB; 21.1 MB; 506 MB | 18.9 MB; 21.1 MB; 606 MB |
  | 45,000 texts of 1,000 characters, each its own (45 MB unpacked, 0.3 MB file) | 43.5 MB; 46.7 MB; 423 MB | 43.6 MB (22 pieces); 46.8 MB; 483 MB |
  | A 10 MB CSV of 100,000 texts of 100 characters | 10.2 MB; 12.9 MB; 114 MB | 10.5 MB; 13.5 MB; 138 MB |

  Leaks (own `.dev` scripts, two parses at a time, counted after a full GC with no parser left): 1,000 parses of NMWC's order file kept the web side's heap at 9-12 MB and its RSS at 100-121 MB (the web side of `93d2331`: 11-21 MB and 140-228 MB); 1,000 parses with the review's file every 10th kept the heap at 9-20 MB, with no parser process, slot or handle left. The web side's longest event-loop stall during a read: 48-272 ms before (the most for the review's file), 49-249 ms after (even for NMWC's four-row late-orders file it was about 50 ms). The parser's peak memory is up to 100 MB higher while it makes the answer, but its heap does not peak there: the smallest heap cap at which a file is still read is the same before and after (320 MB for 50,000 x 49 as CSV, 176 MB for NMWC's shape at the row limit, 144 MB for the 45 MB text file), so no file that was read is refused now. The most a file within the caps can send is about its own text (at most 50 MB unpacked, 10 MB for a CSV) plus 8 bytes a cell (2.5 million cells at most: 20 MB), far below 128 MB; the largest measured is the 45 MB text file's 43.6 MB. (Corrected in the P5 second review.) The "after" heap figure for 50,000 x 49 as CSV (21.1 MB) is not what this fix gives: its `ReplyCollector` built each row one key at a time, and V8 keeps a row that gets more than about 16 keys that way as a hash table, so the web side of `28442ce` held 151 MB for that file (measured again, the same on Node 22 and 24); NMWC's 8-15 columns stay under that line. And the answer's byte limit did not bound the rows the web process builds: a CSV sent as text had no column cap, so a 9.4 MB CSV of one row of a million columns crossed as 16 MB and held 71 MB here. The P5 second review fixes both.
- *Checked by hand.* `pnpm build` and the build check passed. Under `next start` on port 3008 the startup check passed, and so did the upload integration specs (`upload-isolation`, `customers-import`, `orders`, `location-rule-db`: 26 tests). The review's file sent to the customer import (Validate only) was read (50,000 rows) in 0.6-0.8 s; the web process went from 156 to 158 MB, and `/api/health/live` answered in 3 ms (median), 48 ms at most. NMWC's customer and order files were read as before (80 and 373 rows), and NMWC's shape at the row limit in 5.3 s, with `/api/health/live` at most 423 ms. Under `next dev`, started through pnpm, the same integration specs passed; `upload-isolation` failed once, because `next dev` compiled `/api/health/live` again in the middle of it (10.7 s, a pause of the development server only), and passed when run again.
- *The handbook said that `next dev` answers 500 on every API route* (low). It does only when `next` is started without pnpm's launcher, which puts `node_modules/.pnpm/node_modules` on `NODE_PATH` (checked on port 3008: through `pnpm --filter @routeiq/web dev`, `/api/health/live`, `/api/health` and `/api/auth/csrf` answer 200 and the log has no `@sentry/core` error; started as `node node_modules/next/dist/bin/next dev`, 500 and "Can't resolve '@sentry/core'" three times). **Corrected** in [5.2](#52-local-development) (a gotcha: start Next.js through pnpm) and in the P5 block above.
- *Handbook text about the current upload flow still described it as before P5* (low). [3.3](#33-step-1-upload-and-validate-the-order-file) step 1 ended by saying that the parse runs on the event loop with no timeout and that a worker is still to come; the A1 question in [7.5](#75-open-questions-for-the-owner) said web-page and XML "Excel" files are refused "until the worker of assessment PR 5" (they are still refused: the parser process runs the same checks); the tables in [3.2](#32-the-day-at-a-glance) and [3.16](#316-api-route-map-for-the-dispatch-flow) named `parseUpload` for `POST /api/orders/upload`. **Corrected:** step 1 now describes the parser process and the answer limit, the question says these files are still refused and asks whether to accept them now, and both tables name `parseUploadIsolated`.
- **Behaviour changes a dispatcher can see.** None for any file measured: every answer, message, warning, row number and sheet choice is the same (`upload-parse-identity.spec.ts`, 56 cases, unchanged, now through the new answer). A file whose answer would pass 128 MB is refused "needs too much memory"; no file within the upload caps comes near that.
- **Deviations** (each with its reason): *every text crosses once*, which the finding did not ask for: with a size limit alone, a legal file that shows the same longer texts in many cells (an address in 50,000 x 49 cells) would send 100 MB or more and could be refused, where main read it; with each text once the answer is about the file's own text. *The limit is a constant (128 MB), not a setting*, like the upload caps, since no file within the caps comes near it. *A piece is cut once its new texts pass about 4 MB counted as two bytes a character* (the most a character takes), so a piece of plain text is about 2 MB. *The parser stops before it passes the limit*, so the web process never gets more than the limit from it. *The tests' in-process reading* goes through the same messages, so every existing upload spec now also runs the new answer.
- **Tests** (the new ones were run before the fix and failed: the review's file held 199.6 MB here, the others failed on the missing size cut, limit, refusal and constant): `upload-parse-process.spec.ts` +5: the review's file holds under 32 MB here, crosses as under 1 MB and gives the same rows as `parseUpload`; an answer over a 1 MB limit is refused by the parser before it sends that much, and the same file is read under the default limit; a stand-in that sends 1 MB pieces without end (`tests/fixtures/upload-parser/flood.cjs`, new) is stopped here after 2 pieces at a 3 MB limit and killed; the limit is 128 MB; pieces are cut by size, and a text 50,000 rows hold crosses once. Changed on purpose: the stand-in `echo.cjs` answers in the new format, and `lastUploadParse()` also reports the bytes received and whether an answer was too large. Unchanged and passing: `upload-parse-identity.spec.ts` (56), `upload-parse-routes.spec.ts` (13), `build-hardening.spec.ts` (22; its smoke test now reads through `ReplyCollector`), the whole web unit suite, and the upload integration specs. Counts: [5.3](#53-test-suites).
- **Not in this part:** adding `@sentry/core` as a direct dependency of `apps/web`, so that a `next` started without pnpm works too. The owner questions of the P5 block are unchanged; the 128 MB limit is added to them ([7.5](#75-open-questions-for-the-owner)).

**P5 second review fixes** (same branch, `audit-p5-upload-worker`, on `28442ce`). Two findings, each confirmed by two skeptics: one medium (code) and one low (handbook). No new or changed API route (the role matrix is unchanged), no schema or migration change, no solver change, no new setting.

- *A CSV upload had no column or cell cap, so one wide row still blocked the web process for 1-2 s and held 70-190 MB of its heap; the answer's byte limit did not catch it* (medium). A1's 200-column and 2,500,000-cell caps applied to Excel only (`sheetRanges`, and the guard for a CSV sent as Excel). A CSV sent as text (Papa Parse; most browsers send a .csv so) had neither, and the protocol never cuts a row across pieces. So a 9.4 MB CSV of one row of a million columns was read, crossed as one 16 MB piece (far under 128 MB), and `ReplyCollector` built one object of a million keys in one go; the order route then spent 1-2 s more on it (`normalizeOrderRows` joins every header into its error text, the legacy hash stringifies the row). A file of 1,000 rows x 4,000 columns held 188 MB. This contradicted "no file can freeze ... the web process" and "a small file cannot fill the web process either" (top of this handbook, [3.3](#33-step-1-upload-and-validate-the-order-file), [5.6](#56-deployment-on-railway), `admin.md`, `RAILWAY_DEPLOYMENT.md`, `SECURITY.md` 10). **Fixed:**
  - *A CSV sent as text has the caps* (`lib/csv.ts`). `parseCsv` refuses a header of more than 200 values before Papa makes a row object: it reads the first 10 lines with the same Papa call without row objects (the same guessed delimiter and line break, the same blank lines skipped, so the first line kept is the header), because Papa spent 2.0 s on a million-name header and 3.4 s renaming a header of 1.5 million repeated names before any check could run; a header after more blank lines is checked once the file is parsed. After the row limit (so every file refused for its rows keeps its message), `checkCsvCells` refuses a line of more than 200 values and more than 2,500,000 values with the header's; a line with more values than its header counts the extra ones Papa keeps in one list. Words: "This file has 1,000,000 columns; at most 200 can be read. Delete the columns you do not need (also empty columns after your data) and upload again.", "Row 3 of this file has 201 columns; ..." (the header being row 1, as the order check numbers rows) and, for the cells, the words of a CSV sent as Excel (one function, `tooManyTextCells` in `lib/workbook-guard.ts`). `MAX_COLS` and `MAX_CELLS` moved to the dependency-free `lib/upload-limits.ts` (`lib/csv.ts` re-exports them).
  - *No row wider than 200 cells and no more than 2,500,000 cells cross* (`lib/upload-parse/protocol.ts`). `answerMessages` stops before a row of more than `MAX_COLS` keys or one that takes the rows past `MAX_CELLS`, with a `too-large` message that names what was passed (`what`: `bytes`, `columns` or `cells`); `ReplyCollector` checks both before it builds a row. Either way the file is refused "This file needs too much memory to read ..." (400, `UPLOAD_OUT_OF_MEMORY`) and the log says which limit ("its answer would pass 200 cells in a row ...", "... 2,500,000 cells ..."); `lastUploadParse()` reports it as `over`. `parseUpload` never gives more, so only a parser that misbehaves meets this.
  - *The rows are kept compact in the web process* (not asked for). `ReplyCollector` built each row one key at a time, and V8 stores an object that gets more than about 16 keys that way as a hash table (about 3 KB a row of 49 keys): the review's own control, a legal CSV of 50,000 rows x 49 columns, held 151 MB, as main did when it read the file itself. It builds each row with `Object.fromEntries`, which defines the same own keys in the same order (an own `"__proto__"` too, as before) and which V8 keeps compact: 21 MB, on Node 22.13.1 and 24.14.1.
- *Measured* (this PC, Node 24.14.1; own scripts outside the repo; each file in a fresh process with the real parser after a warm-up upload; "stall" is the longest gap of a 5 ms timer in the web-side process, "held" its heap after a full GC with the result kept; before = the code of `28442ce`, after = this fix, main = `parseUpload` of `3451f4d` in the process itself; real files: counts and timings only):

  | File | main (in the web process): time = stall, held | Before: answer, time, stall, held, parser peak | After: answer, time, stall, held, parser peak |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | One row of 1,000,000 columns (9.4 MB) | 4.0 s, 62 MB | read, 6.1 s, 1,058 ms, 71 MB, 603 MB; then the order route 1.0 s + 0.6 s | refused (columns), 0.5 s, 52-120 ms, 0.1 MB, 240 MB |
  | One row of 1,500,000 columns under repeated names (5.7 MB) | 6.2 s, 129 MB | read, 10.7 s, 1,561 ms, 134 MB, 762 MB; then 1.9 s + 1.1 s | refused (columns), 0.3-0.4 s, 54-65 ms, 0.1 MB, 128 MB |
  | 1,000 rows x 4,000 columns (7.7 MB) | 1.6 s, 180 MB | read, 2.8 s, 51 ms, 188 MB, 765 MB | refused (columns), 0.1 s, 48-51 ms, 0 MB, 97 MB |
  | 200 rows x 12,500 columns (4.8 MB) | 1.3 s, 146 MB | read, 2.4 s, 57 ms, 150 MB, 597 MB | refused (columns), 0.1-0.2 s, 47-59 ms, 0 MB, 83 MB |
  | 1,000 rows x 5,000 columns (9.6 MB) | - | read, 3.7 s, 51 ms, 188 MB, 780 MB | refused (columns), 0.2 s, 61-74 ms, 0 MB, 107 MB |
  | 12,500 x 200 and 50,000 x 50 (4.8 MB; 2,500,200 and 2,500,050 cells with the header) | - | read, 1.2-1.4 s, 47-50 ms, 148-151 MB, 592-609 MB | refused (cells), 0.5-0.9 s, 46-59 ms, 0.1 MB, 368-377 MB |
  | 12,400 x 200, within the caps (4.7 MB) | 1.0 s, 142 MB | read, 1.3 s, 63 ms, 147 MB, 617 MB | read, 1.3-1.6 s, 45-47 ms, 20 MB, 550-575 MB |
  | 50,000 x 49, within the caps (4.7 MB; 9.4 MB with 46,656 different texts) | 1.2 s, 147 MB | read, 1.2-1.7 s, 45-53 ms, 151-152 MB, 601-645 MB | read, 1.4-2.1 s, 46-54 ms, 21-22 MB, 548-641 MB |
  | NMWC's shape at the row limit, 50,000 x 15 (5.8 MB) | - | read, 0.7 s, 50 ms, 29 MB, 289 MB | read, 0.7-0.8 s, 45-47 ms, 26 MB, 257-287 MB |
  | NMWC's real files (three order .csv, one sent as Excel, the order .xlsx, late orders, customers, products, trucks) | - | read (373 x 5, 4, 80, 46, 13 rows), 0.11-0.19 s, 42-53 ms, 0.1 MB, 58-66 MB | the same rows, 0.11-0.19 s, 44-93 ms, 0.1 MB, 58-66 MB |

  The order route's own work on the rows of the widest legal files (the legacy hash of the rows, unchanged since before P5): 0.1-0.2 s.
- *Checked in production mode.* `pnpm build` and the build check passed (the parser bundle 838 KB from 10 inputs; no Server Actions, no public source maps). With `NODE_ENV=production` the bundle `.next/upload-parser/parse.cjs` refused the million-column CSV (0.6 s), a row of 201 values and 12,500 x 200 (2,500,200 cells), and read 50,000 x 49 (parser peak 542 MB) and NMWC's order file (373 rows). `next start -p 3008`: the startup check logged `[upload-parse] the file reader works (startup check)`, `/api/health/live` answered in 7 ms, and the upload integration specs (`upload-isolation`, `customers-import`, `orders`, `location-rule-db`: 26 tests) passed against it.
- *The handbook said `tests/setup.ts` is an empty placeholder* (low, docs). Since P5 it makes every spec read uploads in the test process (the `__routeiqUploadParseInProcess` hook, honoured outside production only), which [5.3](#53-test-suites)'s Vitest paragraph contradicted. **Corrected** there (and `useRealUploadParser()` named); `repo-guards.spec.ts` now fails when that paragraph calls it a placeholder or leaves the hook out.
- *Corrected claims:* the audit status line at the top, [2.8](#28-timeouts-budgets-and-limits), [3.3](#33-step-1-upload-and-validate-the-order-file) step 1, [5.6](#56-deployment-on-railway) (the web process holds 20-26 MB for the largest legal files, not "at most 128 MB"), notes in the P5 block (the "under 120 ms" stall) and the P5 review block (its 21.1 MB for 50,000 x 49 was 151 MB at `28442ce`), `SECURITY.md` 10, `admin.md`, `RAILWAY_DEPLOYMENT.md`, and the dispatcher guide (the caps hold for CSV files; "50,000 rows of 50 columns" is over the cell cap with its header row, 49 is not, also in 7.5).
- **Behaviour changes a dispatcher can see.** A CSV sent as text with a header or a line of more than 200 values, or more than 2,500,000 values with the header's, is refused with the messages above; before it was read (or, on a slow PC, refused as too long or too much memory). This includes 50,000 rows of 50 columns (2,500,050 cells), which the same data as .xlsx or as CSV sent as Excel already was. Empty values count, so a CSV saved with more than 200 columns, most of them empty after the data, is refused while the same file sent as Excel is read (owner question, [7.5](#75-open-questions-for-the-owner)). NMWC's files (8-15 columns) are read exactly as before; every other answer, message, warning, row number and sheet choice is the same (`upload-parse-identity.spec.ts`, 56 cases, unchanged).
- **Deviations** (each with its reason): *the header is checked before Papa makes row objects* (the finding proposed `meta.fields` after the parse): the parse itself took 2-3.4 s on the review's headers, in the parser; *the line and cell checks come after the row limit*, so a file refused for its rows keeps its message; *both processes check both limits* (the finding said the parser's answer or the web process): the parser so that the web process never gets such rows, the web process in case a parser sends them anyway; *the rows are built with `Object.fromEntries`* (not asked for): the review's own control within every cap held 151 MB of the web heap; *the defence-in-depth refusal is "needs too much memory"*, as the finding asked, not a column message: only a misbehaving parser can meet it.
- **Tests** (each new one was run before its fix and failed, except the two guards named): `upload-limits.spec.ts` +7 (Papa's calls recorded): 200 / 201 header columns, the 1.5 million-name header refused before a row object is made, a header after 12 blank lines, a row of 201 and of 1,000,000 values with its row number and 200 read with Papa's warning, 2,500,000 / 2,500,100 cells, 250 columns of which 248 empty (refused as text, read as Excel); the guard that 50,001 rows of 50 columns are still "Too many rows" passed before too. `upload-parse-process.spec.ts` +5: the million-column CSV refused by the real parser with this process holding under 16 MB and stalling under 1 s (before: read, 71 MB), the protocol's limits on both sides, 50,000 x 49 holding under 48 MB here (151 MB before), a stand-in that sends a row of 250,000 keys (`tests/fixtures/upload-parser/wide.cjs`, new) refused "needs too much memory"; the guard that a row comes back with its keys in order, an own `"__proto__"` and the same prototype passed with the old and the new way of building rows. `repo-guards.spec.ts` +1 (the Vitest paragraph; it failed on the old sentence). Unchanged and passing: `upload-parse-identity.spec.ts` (56), `upload-parse-routes.spec.ts` (13), the whole web unit suite. Counts: [5.3](#53-test-suites).
- **Not in this part:** the order route's own work on a large legal file (unchanged since before P5); dropping a text CSV's empty columns after the data (owner question).

### 7.5 Open questions for the owner

These cannot be answered from the repository. An AI reviewer should list them as questions, not as findings.

**Production configuration (lives in the Railway dashboard, not in git):**

- Is the `web` service pinned to exactly one replica, and which commit is live?
- (Moot since PR5: the web no longer routes; `OSRM_URL` on `web` does nothing.) Does production OSRM run the image's `MAX_TABLE_SIZE` 1000? Then set `OSRM_TABLE_TILE=1000` on `solver` so a day needs one routing call.
- Does production set `NEXTAUTH_SECRET` or `AUTH_SECRET`? The code never names it; `.env.example`, CI and the Railway runbook say `NEXTAUTH_SECRET`, but next-auth v5 beta prefers `AUTH_SECRET`.
- (Audit PR4, owner decision 18: email reset stays off until audit PR4 lands; after it, per-user serialization makes it safe to switch on.) Is `RESEND_API_KEY` (with a verified `RESEND_FROM`) set? Since PR1, without it production sends no reset emails and logs no links (`/forgot` says so; tenant admins use "Reset password" on the Users screen); search older logs for `[password-reset] dev fallback` and treat links from the last 24 h as exposed.
- Is a separate `JANITOR_TOKEN` set on web (required in production since PR1 for the manual route)? Does any external cron call `/api/cron/janitor`?
- Which header does Railway's edge set for the client IP (`TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS` / `CLIENT_IP_HEADER`)? Check a LOGIN audit row after the PR1 deploy.
- Is `SUPER_ADMIN_EMAILS` set only to addresses the owner controls? (Sign-up stays open by owner decision; it never grants SUPER_ADMIN since PR1.) Was production ever seeded with the deleted `seed-nmwc.ts` demo password? If `admin@nmwc.test` exists, deactivate it.
- Is Railway "Wait for CI" enabled for `web` and `solver`? Do they have watch paths, or does every push to `main` rebuild both?
- Were the PR #27 and PR #29 migrations applied in production with a manual backup taken before each? (`docs/RAILWAY_DEPLOYMENT.md` was last checked on 24 Sep.)
- Does production Postgres (`postgres-ssl:18`) have PostGIS?
- Has anyone decided whether to keep or remove the Postgres public TCP proxy?
- Is any monitoring or alerting configured (on `/api/health` - since audit PR4 alert on `ok: false`, `dispatch.reason` says why -, or `routing.status` not `up` for 10 minutes)? Is `SENTRY_DSN` set?
- Has a restore from the Railway backups ever been tested? Is the planned `pg_dump`-to-R2 job with a quarterly restore drill scheduled?
- What killed Postgres on 12 May 2026?
- What are the solver service's memory and CPU (vCPU) limits? Each request can spawn up to three OR-Tools worker processes, and each CP-SAT repack uses 2 workers; the benchmark machine had 6 cores / 12 threads.
- Does production OSRM run the image default `MAX_TABLE_SIZE` (1000) and the current extract?
- Does a staging environment exist? `CLAUDE.md` §14 calls for `routeiq-staging`; `docs/RAILWAY_DEPLOYMENT.md` documents only production.
- When was `SOLVER_TOKEN` last rotated (policy: every 90 days)?
- Which `ortools` and `pyvrp` versions actually run? `requirements.txt` gives ranges and there is no Python lockfile.
- Which `TenantConfig` values does NMWC production use (shift start and maximum, overtime, loading and unloading minutes per case)? Have the real payloads, kg per case and receiving hours been entered for the `nmlj` test company?
- Who maintains the planning values that no UI or API can edit (the DB-only settings in [3.12](#312-settings-that-change-the-plan), per-truck `tripCost`, `kmPerLitre`, `maxTripsPerDay`, availability, and every `CustomerTypeProfile` row)?
- How is the portable Postgres on port 5433 started on the maintainer's machine? `.dev/` has no start script for it.
- (P5) What are the `web` service's memory limit and its usual and peak memory use (Railway → web → Metrics)? Since P5 each file being read is a separate process of up to about 512 MB + 150 MB, two at once ([5.6](#56-deployment-on-railway)). How fast are the web service's CPUs? The 15 s time limit was measured against this PC.

**Product and design decisions:**

- (Third review of audit PR4.) A `SOLVER_URL` that answers with a redirect is now `degraded` `SOLVER_URL_REDIRECTS` (HTTP 200, `ok: false`: the deploy goes ahead and monitoring alerts), like a 403 from something in front of the solver. Every optimization fails while it lasts. Should it block the deploy (503) instead? Owner decision 5 names only a missing token, the solver's 401 and "not configured" as definite faults, so the fix did not go further.
- (Decided in PR3: refused as 409 `NOTHING_TO_PLAN` before any version is created, and the Re-plan / OPTIMIZE buttons are disabled.) Should a re-plan be allowed when every order is already on frozen loads (L13)?
- (Decided in PR1: VIEWER keeps plan detail and the Excel workbook; the debug JSON is SUPERVISOR+, the audit API TENANT_ADMIN.) Should the Excel export be limited to PLANNER and above instead?
- Should COMPLETED loads mark orders DELIVERED? Is `RunStatus.ARCHIVED` ever meant to be set?
- Should the legacy `/upload` page, `/runs/new` and the dashboard buttons that point to them stay? The `/upload` page is the only place a confirmed batch can be deleted.
- `MANUAL_ADJUSTMENT` is accepted by the replan API but sent by no screen: planned feature or leftover?
- When will the legacy PyVRP path, `test_solver.py`, `scripts/bench_day1.py` and the uncalled web functions be removed (`OPTIMIZER_BENCHMARK.md` §6.4)? Are pre-Sep 2026 legacy runs still viewed or exported in production?
- (Decided in PR5: no factor on estimated cells, L21; the driver is paid for the whole truck day, L22.) Should a `ReconciliationError` map to a clear HTTP status (L24)?
- Are `UNKNOWN_CUSTOMER`, `UNKNOWN_PRODUCT`, `LOCKED_PLAN_CONFLICT`, `ROUTING_PROVIDER_FAILURE` (as a reason code) and `UNKNOWN` meant to be emitted somewhere?
- (Decided in PR5: the search time is automatic; the setting is gone from Settings.) Should `early_preference_per_min` and `change_penalty_per_stop` be tenant-configurable? (They are listed as planner rules on Settings' effective values panel.)
- Does the assumption that the first load of a day (without frozen loads) is loaded before the shift starts match NMWC operations? (Since the PR8 review it applies only to a plan made before its delivery day; a plan made on the day counts the loading from now.)
- (Decided by the owner when PR8 was merged: same-day plans start from now + the turnaround between loads, and on a plan made on the delivery day the loading per case of every new load counts from now, also on a truck standing at the depot.) Still open: is the turnaround the right preparation time for a late order at NMWC (picking, loading, paperwork), or should it be its own setting (an additive column)? Should the dispatcher be able to type a "plan from" time instead of the clock? Can some loading of a late order already have started before the plan is made at NMWC (today that load should be locked or marked Loading first)?
- Should the 80 % vs 100 % search turnaround estimate be revisited once real NMWC loading times exist?
- (Decided by the owner on 27 Sep 2026, evening; audit PR A5) **(A2) Should a LOW-confidence location reading require the dispatcher to move the pin before saving?** Owner: "its a standing rule locations should always be correct as part of sop no item will be delivered without location." A standing rule the system enforces, not a report: the location part of PR A5 ([7.4](#74-review-leads-found-while-writing-this-handbook), [3.4](#34-step-2-customer-locations-and-details)). Enforced: a reading that is not exact is saved only as a pin placed by hand (ADD LOCATION and the customer page's Set location, and the server checks it again); no other route stores such a reading as a usable location (the customer import leaves it out and lists it); a LOW location nobody confirmed is not planned; a legacy run is not dispatched while a customer on it has no correct location. Decided by the owner on 28 Sep 2026: **an imported location nobody has confirmed yet (HIGH or MEDIUM, "Location came from an import and was never confirmed by a dispatcher") is still planned, with that note** (owner: "Plan with a warning": NMWC's customer file counts as correct, and a pin a dispatcher corrects becomes confirmed); a MEDIUM one can no longer be confirmed as it is (the dispatcher places the pin). Customers confirmed at a point outside the delivery area before A5 are still planned: the read-only count in [5.12](#512-how-to-verify-a-deploy) check 13 lists them for a look on the map. Decided by the owner on 28 Sep 2026 (asked after the A5 third review): **should a load be refused whenever a customer's pin was corrected by more than 50 m after it was planned, until it is re-planned?** Owner: **"Allow, driver gets new pin"**. A pin corrected after planning does not block the load: it can go, and the driver's text and sheet show the planned stop with "New pin - ask the dispatcher which one to use" and a link to the new pin (the frozen-plan-facts behaviour since PR4, kept as it is; recorded in [7.4](#74-review-leads-found-while-writing-this-handbook), "A5 owner decisions"). Only a stop still planned at a point that was not usable (LOW, outside the area, 0,0) when a new pin more than 50 m away replaced it is refused (`STOP_PIN_REPLACED`, [3.8](#38-step-6-load-states)): that point was never a place to deliver to.
- (Decided by the owner on 27 Sep 2026, evening; audit PR A5) **(A3) Do you want an optional read-only check for orders or upload batches that have no depot (NULL `depotId`)?** Owner: "no, all orders must have depots linked to them". Enforced: migration `20260930120000_orders_always_have_depot` gives every such order and file a depot and makes `depotId` required; an order file is never linked to a depot nobody chose ([7.4](#74-review-leads-found-while-writing-this-handbook), [5.5](#55-database-migrations)). Still open from it: should the legacy Upload orders page get a depot choice (today a company with two or more active depots uploads on Daily dispatch only)? Should the orders kept on a history-only depot `NO-DEPOT` be movable to a real depot on a screen (today they are history only; production is expected to have none)?
- (Decided by the owner on 28 Sep 2026; asked after the A5 fifth review) The customer import counts one zero at the end of a coordinate, in every file: 23.5800 is 3 decimals and not saved as exact (a file cannot tell a rough 23.58 shown with 4 decimals from a typed one; about 1 in 100 real 4-decimal values per axis ends in 00 and then needs its pin placed by hand). **Should ADD LOCATION and the new-customer form (`POST /api/customers`) count the same way?** Owner: **"Same rule everywhere"**. Done in audit PR A5 ([3.4](#34-step-2-customer-locations-and-details); [7.4](#74-review-leads-found-while-writing-this-handbook), "A5 owner decisions"): a pair a dispatcher types or pastes into ADD LOCATION (the Read, and the server's check on Save) or sends to `POST /api/customers` counts one zero at the end of each coordinate, with the import's own function (`countedText` in `location-input.ts`). "23.5800, 58.4100" needs a pin placed by hand, and the dialog and the server say why: "Fewer than 4 decimals (only one zero at the end counts). Drop the pin on the customer's exact location."; "23.5850, 58.4105" and 6-decimal pairs are exact as before. A pair written as text in a Google Maps link or a `geo:` URI counts the same way, because padding reaches it; Google's own pin (`!3d...!4d...`) keeps its digits, not cut, but needs 4 decimals (A5 sixth review: Google repeats a coordinate searched for as the pin, so a searched "23.58, 58.41" came back exact), and degrees, minutes and seconds of a pair with fewer than 4 decimals need a pin too. Before, a dispatcher who typed or pasted "23.5800, 58.4100" (for example copied from a cell formatted to show 4 decimals) got an exact reading, saved as confirmed.
- (A5 sixth review) **Customers a dispatcher confirmed from a reading that is not exact under today's rules: flag them, or leave them?** ADD LOCATION keeps the text a location was read from, so they can be found: a pair padded with zeros ("23.5800, 58.4100", before the "Same rule everywhere" change), a rough coordinate searched in Google Maps and shared (before the sixth review), and any reading the screen before A5 let through (a map centre, fewer than 4 decimals, a directions link read at its start). Today they stay usable and are planned, because a confirmed point is never judged again. Flagging them would mean their orders are not planned until a dispatcher places the pin by hand. `apps/web/prisma/check-saved-readings.ts` counts them per company, read-only ([5.12](#512-how-to-verify-a-deploy) step 13): the question is about its "confirmed from a pasted reading ... read as not exact today" count only, where the saved point is where the text kept points (A5 seventh review). The customers it lists as "not judged by their text" were not located from that text: a map click or two typed numbers on the customer page before A5 (which set the point, confirmed and HIGH, and left the text as it was), or a saved point confirmed as it is; flagging by the text would stop exact map-clicked customers. The same lines say how many of those have fewer than 4 decimals as stored (typed rough on the customer page, or exact values ending in 0; the stored number cannot say which), for a look on the map, like the unconfirmed HIGH query in step 13. Nothing changes until the owner decides.
- (Audit PR "Intake and master data", F02; owner decision 6) Can NMWC's ERP export the same sales order and product on two rows? RouteIQ adds them together (highest priority, every note, a warning); NMWC IT should confirm whether that happens and why.
- (A1 review, applied as defaults) Upload caps beyond decision 16: at most 200 columns per sheet, 2,500,000 cells (50,000 rows of 49 columns with the header row; since the P5 second review for a CSV sent as text too), links over 200,000 cells and 10,000 comments; an upload read as Excel must be an .xlsx, an old .xls or CSV. Does NMWC's sales system ever export "Excel" as a web page or XML file (an .xls that Excel opens with a format warning)? Those are still refused after P5: the parser process runs the same checks. Now that a slow file only costs its own process, should RouteIQ accept them? (Nothing changes until the owner decides.) Should the cell cap be 1,000,000 instead (worst case about 4 s and 0.5 GB instead of 9-11 s and 1 GB, but 50,000-row files with more than 20 columns refused)? A formula cell without a saved value now reads as blank (it read as 0): acceptable? (A1 v3, applied as defaults on the same terms) At most 1,000 metadata entries of each kind and 1,000 comment authors per workbook; Excel writes one or two metadata entries, and the person list names the people who wrote threaded comments. (A1 v4, applied as a default on the same terms) A workbook with a chart sheet (a tab that holds only a chart), a dialog sheet or a macro sheet is refused, because SheetJS turns a chart sheet's chart into cells past every cap. Do NMWC's order or customer workbooks ever have a chart sheet? If they do, the alternative is to skip such sheets instead of refusing the file.
- (P5, applied as defaults, to confirm before the merge) **Uploads are read in a separate process, not a worker thread** (the assessment said worker thread): a worker's heap cap can abort the whole web process (reproduced with a legal 0.22 MB file on Node 22 and 24), a child process ends alone. **Heap cap 512 MB and time limit 15 s**: NMWC's real files need about 60-70 MB and well under a second, NMWC's shape at the row limit about 400 MB and 5-20 s on this PC; the widest legal files (50,000 rows of more than about 45 columns, or the same as CSV sent as Excel) need 650-900 MB and are refused, where before P5 they were read while every user waited 20-40 s. Keep 512 MB / 15 s, or 768-1024 MB / 20-30 s (more memory on the web service, see the question above)? **The request body**: `req.formData()` still reads a whole upload request into the web process before the 10 MB check (an older gap, not E2): cap it by `Content-Length` first? **The name P5** in the handbook (A5 is the owner-rules PR). (P5 review, applied as a default) **The rows a parser process sends back are at most 128 MB**, each text sent once; a file whose answer would be larger is refused "needs too much memory". No file within the upload caps comes near it (the largest measured sends 44 MB; NMWC's files well under 1 MB). Keep 128 MB? (P5 second review, applied as a default) **A CSV sent as text counts every value, empty ones too**: a CSV saved from Excel with more than 200 columns, most of them empty columns after the data, is refused ("... (also empty columns after your data) ..."), while the same file sent as Excel (Chrome or Edge on a PC with Excel installed) is read, because SheetJS drops those columns. Should RouteIQ drop empty columns after the data in a text CSV too, as SheetJS does? (Nothing changes until the owner decides; NMWC's CSV files have 8-15 columns.)
- (PR9, bring forward) Should an order brought forward get a higher priority (for example one step up, or P1 after a second carry)? Today it keeps its own priority (no automatic bump); it would be an owner setting. Is 7 days the right window to look back? Should orders on a load that was never marked Dispatched count as not delivered (today: yes, the dispatcher unticks them), and should a carried order's original become a status of its own (today it keeps its status; `carriedToOrderId` marks it)? (PR9 review, decided by the owner) Today's leftovers are offered for tomorrow the same evening: today's orders are listed in their own group, unticked, and carried only when ticked on their own ([7.4](#74-review-leads-found-while-writing-this-handbook), "Owner decision: today's orders").
- (Decided in the PR7 review, T1: no day size may get less automatic search time than before PR7. The schedule reaches 150 s at 200 stops, so 201–299-stop days keep 150 s and 121–200-stop days get more, up to about +3 min wall time at 200 stops.) Is 150 s enough for 201–350-stop days? The re-test's S03 (240 stops) varied 317.7–377.3 km between runs on 150 s; re-running it several times, and once with a longer `time_limit_sec` for comparison, would show whether those days need more.
- (Decided by the owner when PR7 was merged: option (a), keep RECOMMENDED as it is - priority-first timing - now that the options table and the Excel show the trade-off; a cheaper option stays one **Use instead** away.) **(PR7, N1) Should RECOMMENDED become cost-first?** Today it minimises money + preferences: minutes outside preferred windows (0.05 OMR/min), P1/P2 delivered later in the day (0.01 / 0.005 OMR per minute after the shift start) and moved orders on late-order re-plans (3 OMR each). On the Sep 2026 re-test that cost +1.6 to +37.2 OMR a day over the cheapest option, for P1/P2 delivered hours earlier (S05 on main: P1 on average 06:54 against about 09:47). Options: (a) keep it, now that the table shows the trade-off (PR7); (b) cost-first: RECOMMENDED = the cheapest plan, the preferences only as a tie-break (P1/P2 then often in the second wave); (c) keep the preferences but lower the early-delivery rates, or make them tenant settings (see the question on `early_preference_per_min` above). A same-day re-plan should measure "early" from the re-plan time, not 06:00 (N2), whichever is chosen.

**Records that exist only outside the repo:**

- The go-live time, the test company slug `nmlj`, the real-data counts (373 lines, 46 SKUs, 13 trucks, bays × 95 cases, 3 t / 10 t payloads), NMWC's actual 13 trucks / 19 trips, and the production v4/v5 figures are recorded only in the work log. (PR #30's 4 trucks / 12 loads / 229 OMR and the work log's 4 trucks / 12 loads / 884 km / 228 OMR are two different plans, so they do not conflict: the first is the MIN_TRUCKS option shown on v4, the second the v5 plan after re-optimize, per work log.)
- The deep-test result "engine = proven optimum on 27-28 of 30 small exact instances" exists only in the work log / local `.dev/bench`, not in `docs/OPTIMIZER_BENCHMARK.md`.
- PR #30's description was written when the PR was opened (07:17 UTC on 25 Sep) and says an adversarial review "is running on the branch". Commit `6c525ba` (07:20 UTC, merged in PR #30) is labelled "Review finding" and records one finding and its fix: a Re-plan with a late order waiting stays a LATE_ORDER re-plan. The repo does not record whether the review found anything else, or when it finished.
- PR #27's GitHub description names real NMWC customers. Should it be edited, given that real customer names should not leave `.dev/`?

## Glossary

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **NMWC** | National Mineral Water Co., Muscat, Oman: the customer and only production user of RouteIQ |
| **Tenant** | One company in the multi-tenant database (`Tenant` + `TenantConfig`). NMWC's demo tenant slug is `nmwc`; the production real-data test company is `nmlj` |
| **Depot** | The warehouse every load starts from and returns to (`Depot`) |
| **Customer branch** | One delivery location, identified by customer code + branch key (`customerKey(code, branchKey)`) |
| **Order** | All lines for one customer branch on one delivery date (`Order`), made of SKU lines (`OrderLine`) |
| **Stop** | One visit in the solver request: all open orders of one customer branch (`DispatchStop`, `stop_id` = customer id), or one part of a split delivery |
| **Load / trip** | One physical truck departure from the depot and return (`PlanLoad`, solver `PlannedLoad`). A truck can make several loads a day (`maxTripsPerTruck`, `maxTripsPerDay`). "Trip" and "load" mean the same thing |
| **Split delivery / portion / part** | An order too big for any truck, cut into parts (`<customerId>#k` stops, `<orderId>~k` portion ids); `<orderId>~open` is the open rest of a partly frozen order. Portion columns: `portionCases`, `portionWeightKg`, `portionLinesJson` |
| **Frozen load** | A load in LOCKED, LOADING, DISPATCHED or COMPLETED. It is never changed by a re-plan; the solver sees it as `frozen_trips` |
| **Load states** | PLANNED → LOCKED → LOADING → DISPATCHED → COMPLETED (`lib/dispatch/load-state.ts`) |
| **Plan (RunPlan)** | One version of the plan for one depot and one delivery date |
| **Plan version** | Each re-plan creates version N+1 (`createNextVersion`) with a reason (INITIAL, LATE_ORDER, MANUAL_ADJUSTMENT, REOPTIMIZE); the parent becomes SUPERSEDED and read-only |
| **Scenario / plan option** | One of three solver answers: RECOMMENDED (service, then true cost), MIN_TRUCKS (fewest trucks and loads), MIN_DISTANCE (fewest km). `ScenarioResult` rows |
| **Applied / chosen scenario** | The scenario turned into loads (`RunPlan.chosenScenarioId`). RECOMMENDED is applied automatically; "Use instead" applies another |
| **Re-plan vs re-optimize** | A re-plan creates a new version and keeps frozen loads. REOPTIMIZE is a re-plan with no moving charge (every stop may change truck). LATE_ORDER and MANUAL_ADJUSTMENT re-plans keep the moving charge. A REOPTIMIZE while late orders wait becomes LATE_ORDER |
| **Plan continuity / moving charge** | `previous_truck_id` per stop plus `change_penalty_per_stop` (3 OMR): moving a stop to another truck costs extra (`usesPlanContinuity`) |
| **Late order** | An order recorded after the planning cutoff (default 18:00 the day before, Asia/Muscat) or after a plan is applied (`Order.isLate`) |
| **Pending order** | An order of the day that is not in the applied plan's scope, i.e. it arrived after the plan was made |
| **Unserved** | An order or part left out of a scenario, always with a reason code (`UnservedOrder`) |
| **Pre-drop** | An order unserved before the solver runs (missing or invalid location) |
| **Reconciliation** | The exact cases check: uploaded = planned + unserved, in total, per SKU, per sales order and per line (`reconcile()`; solver `_assert_reconciled`). Dispatch is refused unless it is OK |
| **P1-P5** | Priorities: P1 highest, P5 lowest. **Strict priorities** mean one higher-priority stop outweighs any number of lower ones |
| **Hard / preferred window** | Receiving hours. Service must start inside the hard window; the preferred window is a soft cost |
| **Service time** | Minutes at the customer: base minutes plus unloading minutes per case (`stopServiceMin`) |
| **Turnaround / reload** | Time at the depot between loads: `reloadMinutes` plus loading minutes per case |
| **Planned from (same-day plan)** | A plan built on its own delivery day starts from now + `reloadMinutes` (the preparation time) instead of the first departure: no new load leaves earlier (PR8, `lib/dispatch/plan-from.ts`) |
| **Loading from (same-day plan)** | The time a plan built on its own delivery day was made (`loading_from_min`): loading of its new loads starts then, so each leaves no earlier than it + turnaround + loading per case of its cases, on every truck (PR8 review) |
| **Truck-day** | The solver's per-truck availability: earliest departure, latest return, trips left (`TruckDay`) |
| **Reload node** | An optional OR-Tools node at the depot that ends one load and starts the next on the same truck |
| **Repack** | The CP-SAT post-solve stage that re-assigns whole loads to trucks and departure times (`load_repack.py`) |
| **Warm start** | Starting the MIN_TRUCKS and MIN_DISTANCE searches from RECOMMENDED's raw loads |
| **OSRM** | Open Source Routing Machine: the self-hosted road-routing server (`infra/osrm`) that gives real road km and times |
| **Haversine / Estimated** | Straight-line distance × 1.3, used when OSRM is not configured or fails; plans are labelled "Estimated km" |
| **roadTimeFactor** | Multiplier (default 1.25) that turns OSRM car times into truck times |
| **RunJob** | One optimize attempt with its request, response and error JSON |
| **In-flight map** | The in-memory registry of running optimize jobs (`trackInflight`); the reason web must run as one replica |
| **Janitor** | The 60-second sweep that fails jobs stuck for more than 15 minutes and closes stale driver shifts |
| **Legacy plan** | A plan made by the old PyVRP optimizer before Sep 2026; read-only (`LEGACY_PLAN`) |
| **Driver sheets / driver pack** | The PDF per load for drivers (`driver-pack.tsx`); no costs or margins |
| **OMR** | Omani rial, the currency of all costs |
| **OPERATION-PROJECT Lane A** | NMWC's separate system that records actual deliveries; RouteIQ only plans ([`docs/OPERATION_PROJECT_HANDOFF.md`](OPERATION_PROJECT_HANDOFF.md)) |
| **Ayun IVMS** | NMWC's vehicle-tracking system, a source of actual trip times for calibrating `roadTimeFactor` |
| **`.dev/`** | The git-ignored local folder on the maintainer's machine with real data, a portable Postgres and the benchmark harness. Never copy it |

## Document map

Every Markdown document in the repo (outside `node_modules`, the solver venv and `.dev/`).

| File | What it is |
|---|---|
| `docs/PROJECT_HANDBOOK.md` | This handbook: the whole project in one place |
| [`docs/SECURITY.md`](SECURITY.md) | Security model after stabilization PR1 (sessions, sign-in, sign-up, platform admins, role matrix, secrets, retired driver app) and the owner runbook for deploying it |
| [`docs/DISPATCHER_GUIDE.md`](DISPATCHER_GUIDE.md) | Daily workflow for dispatchers (upload → resolve → optimize → review → lock/export/dispatch → late orders) |
| [`docs/OPTIMIZER_DESIGN.md`](OPTIMIZER_DESIGN.md) | How the optimizer decides, in plain language: capacity, P1-P5, windows, multi-load trucks, cost. The behavioural spec to check the code against |
| [`docs/OPTIMIZER_BENCHMARK.md`](OPTIMIZER_BENCHMARK.md) | Engine choice (OR-Tools vs PyVRP, VROOM, Timefold, Google), benchmarks, post-solve validation and review fixes (§8) |
| [`docs/OSRM_SETUP.md`](OSRM_SETUP.md) | OSRM production runbook: image, Railway and VM options, monthly map refresh, rollback, monitoring, truck-time calibration |
| [`docs/RAILWAY_DEPLOYMENT.md`](RAILWAY_DEPLOYMENT.md) | Railway services and dashboard settings, cut-over and rollback, backups, the May 2026 Postgres outage |
| [`docs/NMWC_DISPATCH_RESTART_AUDIT.md`](NMWC_DISPATCH_RESTART_AUDIT.md) | Audit of the code recovered on 24 Sep 2026: what worked, what was broken, what changed |
| [`docs/OPERATION_PROJECT_HANDOFF.md`](OPERATION_PROJECT_HANDOFF.md) | Future integration contract with OPERATION-PROJECT Lane A (design only, not built) |
| [`docs/LOCAL_DEV.md`](LOCAL_DEV.md) | Local setup, with Windows notes; preferred over the README's older steps |
| [`docs/admin.md`](admin.md) | Admin runbook (partly v1-era): tenant and platform-admin lifecycle, password resets, failure triage, token rotation, rate limits (upload limit out of date), single-replica rule, backup plan, migration rules |
| [`README.md`](../README.md) | Entry point; links to `docs/`. Its "Local development" and "Deploying" sections are older than `docs/` |
| [`CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md) | **Historical:** the original May 2026 v1.3 multi-tenant SaaS build spec. Superseded wherever it disagrees with this handbook, `docs/` or the code (for example, receiving windows and split deliveries are implemented) |
| [`OVERNIGHT_REPORT.md`](../OVERNIGHT_REPORT.md) | **Historical:** the 11-12 May 2026 build and bug-hunt report from the PyVRP era; some claims are wrong (see the restart audit) |
| [`apps/solver/README.md`](../apps/solver/README.md) | Solver service: endpoints, token, local run and tests |
| [`apps/web/prisma/synth-data/README.md`](../apps/web/prisma/synth-data/README.md) | The May 2026 synthetic NMWC dataset; refers to a generator script that is not in the repo |
| [`apps/web/tests/fixtures/README.md`](../apps/web/tests/fixtures/README.md) | Test fixtures; mentions files that no longer exist |
| [`apps/web/tests/fixtures/nmwc/README.md`](../apps/web/tests/fixtures/nmwc/README.md) | Generated sample daily sales-order files in the NMWC ERP export shape (from `fixtures:dispatch`; never hand-edited) |
| [`.github/PULL_REQUEST_TEMPLATE.md`](../.github/PULL_REQUEST_TEMPLATE.md) | PR template: test commands and the tenant-isolation checklist |
| `.env.example` | Not Markdown. Placeholders for the main web and solver variables (`DATABASE_URL`, `NEXTAUTH_*`, `SOLVER_URL` / `SOLVER_TOKEN`, `OSRM_URL`, `JANITOR_TOKEN`, `MAPBOX_TOKEN`, `RESEND_API_KEY`, `SUPER_ADMIN_EMAILS`, `SENTRY_DSN`; `SOLVER_PARALLEL`, `RATE_LIMITS_DISABLED`, `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS`, `CLIENT_IP_HEADER`, `RESEND_FROM`, `AUTH_URL`, `SIGNUP_MODE` and `PORT` commented out). It is not complete: [2.9](#29-environment-variables-by-service) is the full list |
